- **Policy drift.** `privacy.html` (D-6), SECURITY.md and the Data safety draft say "on a computer" and "not written while the garden lock is on". Step 8 ships with the code, after approval.
- **First-load room.** 43 of 234 B, with D-31 and 4.0's own items also waiting; WP-F 3 would leave about 2.6 kB.
- **W-4** (a dropped tab still writing the file after a delete) is unverified. Check it with the two-tab setup first.
- **Scope creep** toward writing while the app is closed. That would mean reading IndexedDB outside the WebView, so it stays out.

**Questions for the owner.**

- **D-76.** What should a kept sealed file carry: both ways in (passcode and recovery code, the existing form that every build since 27 October 2026 opens), or the recovery code only (safe from offline guessing, but a new form today's `readLockedCopy` refuses)? *Recommendation:* both, with the passcode advice beside it. Say that a longer passcode keeps it safer; never require one. D-23, PBKDF2 or Argon2id, is a separate question.
- **D-77.** Build it after 4.0 reaches production (P-22), the desktop half first, with new privacy and SECURITY.md wording approved as D-6 was, and about 43 B of the Android first load spent on purpose (D-10, P-18)? *Recommendation:* yes.
- **D-78.** Should the card name the places it recognises, Google Drive and its 30-day history included? And should P-20 check whether today's setting already shows in Chrome on Android? *Recommendation:* yes to both, plainly and once. If it shows, keep it, with honest wording.

### c.3 A letter to open later

**In one line.** The gardener writes a letter to their future self and chooses when it comes back (in a month, a season, a year, or on a chosen day), and Marigold brings it back on that day; until then it stays sealed on the device and is never quoted anywhere else.

**Recommendation:** build later, in the first release after 4.0, because 4.0 needs the Android first load's spare room first (P-18) · **Effort:** M · **First load:** +104 B gzip on the Android build (`dist-android`), measured in a scratch copy with `vite build --mode android` and the gzip-9 sum `scripts/check-budgets.mjs` uses. That left 130 B of the 234 B spare at the time of measuring (a.2 found about 245 B). Widening `Route.letter` adds an estimated 10 to 20 B, not measured. +136 B on the web build · **Waits on:** D-6 (one new privacy-policy bullet), D-10 (an Android baseline set on purpose, if 4.0 has used the room), D-79, D-80

**Why it matters.** The garden already writes to the gardener every Sunday (Marigold's letter, `lib/summaries.ts`) and looks back through "On this day". The gardener cannot write forward: say something now, and hear it back months later from the person they were. A paper time capsule only works if you remember it is there. The garden can remember the day for them. The best-fitting evidence is about a keepsake, not health. In time-capsule studies, people underestimated how curious they would be to re-read ordinary moments they had recorded, and how much they would enjoy it, including after seven months ([Zhang et al. 2014](https://www.psychologicalscience.org/journals/psychological-science/0956797614542274/)). The wellbeing evidence is modest. In one online study, 738 Japanese adults in April 2020 wrote one letter to or from their self a year ahead. Negative affect fell (d = 0.44 and 0.47) and positive affect rose (d = 0.30 and 0.36) against a control. It was measured only straight afterwards, and the authors say they do not know how long it lasts ([Chishima, Liu & Wilson 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8013690/)). Other results are weaker or negative:

- In Rutchick et al. (2018), students who wrote to themselves 20 years ahead reported more exercise over the next days (an estimated 12.9 against 9.3 minutes a day). The 3-month letter, which is this app's "season", was the *control*. The outcome was self-reported, and no letter was ever given back ([paper](https://www.columbia.edu/~ms4992/Pubs/2018_Rutchick-Slepian-Reyes-Pleskus-Hershfield_JEPA.pdf)).
- In Chishima and Wilson's school study, a one-way letter produced no measurable change. Only a letter plus a reply written as the future self raised self-continuity, and a month later there was more career planning ([SPSP](https://spsp.org/news-center/character-context-blog/what-would-your-future-self-tell-you)).
- Two pre-registered direct replications of a letter to the future self, with N = 314 and N = 463 (about 2.5 times the original samples), did not reproduce the original effect on delinquent choices and risky investments ([University of Bonn, 2020](https://bonndoc.ulb.uni-bonn.de/xmlui/handle/20.500.11811/8612?show=full)). The original was [van Gelder, Hershfield & Nordgren (2013)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23592649/).
- Expressive writing in general has a small effect: r = .075 across 146 randomised studies ([Frattaroli 2006](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.132.6.823)). Across 39 RCTs it had no significant long-term effect on depressive symptoms in physically healthy adults ([Reinhold, Bürkner & Holling 2018](https://paul-buerkner.github.io/publications/pdf/2018__Reinhold_et_al__Clinical_Psychology_Science_and_Practice.pdf)). Across 31 RCTs with long follow-up (N = 4,012) the effect was small and delayed, g = −0.12 ([Guo 2023](https://doi.org/10.1111/bjc.12408)). Writing usually raises distress for a short time, so writers should know they can stop at any time ([Baikie & Wilhelm 2005](https://sparq.stanford.edu/sites/g/files/sbiybj19021/files/media/file/baikie_wilhelm_2005_-_emotional_and_physical_health_benefits_of_expressive_writing.pdf)).

So this is a keepsake with, at most, a short lift on the day it is written. It is not a route to lasting wellbeing, and no copy, Guide text or Play listing may claim otherwise. Self-Determination Theory supports the shape: technology helps wellbeing when it supports autonomy, and engagement in itself does not ([Peters, Calvo & Ryan 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5985470)). So the letter waits without nudging. "Write back to yourself" offers the two-way exchange without claiming its effect.

**Where it works.** Nothing new is asked of any platform. The letter is stored in IndexedDB through the existing repository, and the day is picked with `<input type="date">` and `min`/`max`, Baseline widely available since April 2021 ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTML/Reference/Elements/input/date)). The garden already uses that field in `EarlierEntry`, `GoalEditor`, `GoalRow` and `SightingForm`. As of November 2026:

- **The PWA on iPhone and iPad.** It works. A Safari garden that is *not* on the Home Screen can lose all its script-writable storage after seven days of Safari use without a visit. Home Screen web apps keep their own counter ([WebKit, 2020](https://webkit.org/blog/10218/full-third-party-cookie-blocking-and-more/)), and WebKit grants persistence by heuristics ([WebKit storage policy](https://webkit.org/blog/14403/updates-to-storage-policy/)). A letter set a year away is only as safe as the garden around it. `lib/storage.ts` already asks for persistence, and the form says once, where storage is best-effort, that a backup keeps the letter safe.
- **Android Chrome and desktop browsers** (Chromium, Firefox, Safari). It works, with the same durability as the rest of the garden.
- **The Android app** (Capacitor 8, minimum WebView 111 under P-9). It works. Storage is in the app's private area and is not evicted. Nothing under `android/` changes, so the Gradle checks are not needed, but `e2e/android.spec.ts` runs the flow.
- **Read-aloud was researched and left out.** Android WebView deliberately leaves out the Web Speech API ([Chromium change, May 2015](https://codereview.chromium.org/1127363004)). Can I use lists WebView 157 as unsupported in its October 2026 data ([Can I use](https://caniuse.com/speech-synthesis)), and a July 2026 report finds it undefined on Chromium 138 ([Xibo community](https://community.xibo.org.uk/t/android-player-speechsynthesis-web-speech-api-unavailable-in-webview-chromium-138/38219)). A native engine would need a `<queries>` entry for `TTS_SERVICE` ([Android Developers](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/speech/tts/TextToSpeech.Engine)), and its voices can need the network ([Voice](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/speech/tts/Voice)). In browsers, Chrome desktop's Google voices are remote ([Readium](https://readium.org/speech/docs/WebSpeech.html)). Chrome on Android never marks a voice remote ([`tts_android.cc`](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/lkgr/content/browser/speech/tts_android.cc)), so [`localService`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/SpeechSynthesisVoice/localService) cannot prove the words stay on the device. Screen readers already read the letter.

**What the gardener sees.** It lives in the Journal's letters card (`components/journal/Letters.tsx`), and the garden's letter card (`components/garden/LetterCard.tsx`) announces a returned letter. No new page, route path or tab.

1. In the Journal, a folded section, "Letters to yourself": "Write to yourself, and Marigold will bring it back on the day you choose." The button is **Write a letter to open later**.
2. The form, "A letter to open later", says: "Write as much or as little as you like. You can stop at any time; nothing is counted or earned." An optional "A way to begin" offers one opener, with **Another** ("What would you like to remember about now?"). "Your letter" keeps a draft, with the existing "Draft kept". A character count shows only near the 2,000-character limit.
3. "When should it come back?" shows each choice's real date: "In a month: Thursday 17 December", "In a season: Wednesday 17 February", "In a year: Wednesday 17 November 2027", or "On a day I choose" (tomorrow to five years ahead). Beneath: "It stays sealed on this device until then, and it is never quoted anywhere else in the garden." The buttons are **Seal it** and **Not now**, which keeps the draft.
4. Focus moves to "Sealed. Marigold will bring it back on Wednesday 17 February." "Waiting to come back" lists only dates ("Comes back on Wednesday 17 February, written on Tuesday 17 November"), never the words and never a countdown. A ⋯ menu offers **Change the day** and **Let it go**, which asks once, then offers Undo for a few seconds, as goals do.
5. On the day, or the first visit after it, the garden shows the envelope card in the page, never over it: "A letter has come back to you. Marigold has brought back the letter you wrote on 17 November. It will keep in your journal for whenever you'd like it." **Open it** and **Later**; Later puts the card away for good. It comes before the weekly letter's card. On a gentle day it waits until tomorrow. Several at once are named without a number: "Letters you wrote have come back".
6. **Open it** goes to the Journal, with focus on "Written on Tuesday 17 November 2026" and a button, **Open the letter**: opening is a second, deliberate step. Under the words are **Write back to yourself** ("Writing back to my letter of 17 November" offered as the first line) and the quiet "If you need support now" link, as the comfort plan offers it. Opened letters fold under "Opened", newest first.

**What it stores, and what older builds do.**

- **Shape.** One optional field, `Profile.futureLetters?: FutureLetter[]`, absent rather than empty. Each is `{ id, text, writtenOn, opensOn, openedOn?, cardPutAway?: true }`, with `text` trimmed to at most 2,000 characters and the dates local. At most 24 are kept, waiting and opened together; the cap applies when sealing, never on read. It sits in the profile for the reason `comfortPlan` and `outfitPresets` do (`src/types.ts`): older builds write the meta record field by field, but carry the profile through whole.
- **Versions.** `DATABASE_VERSION` stays 8 and `CURRENT_STATE_VERSION` stays 8. No new store; a new collection would have cost an upgrade older builds cannot open.
- **Older builds.** Checked in a scratch copy: HEAD's `validateGarden` carries `profile.futureLetters` through untouched (the spread at the top of `checkProfile`), from storage and through a JSON backup. 3.9 and earlier keep the letters and show nothing. A letter whose day passes on an older build comes back on the first visit with a newer one: late, never lost.
- **Backups (invariant 6).** The envelope is unchanged. The garden inside gains one optional profile field, as it did for the comfort plan and saved looks. No field means no letters. The plain backup carries the words in plain text, as it does every journal entry; the protected backup and the move file seal them.
- **The lock.** The profile is in `meta`, which `repository/sealedStores.ts` seals under its own name, and the `future-letter` draft sits in the sealed drafts record. Nothing changes in `sealedStores.ts` or `gardenSwitch.ts`. The form is draft-backed, so it neither holds the auto-lock (P-13) nor needs `data-lock-loses`.
- **Two tabs.** Sealing, opening, putting away, changing the day and letting go all go through `update()`: one meta write and a broadcast, and other tabs redraw. A stale tab gets `GardenConflictError` and the existing "not kept" notice, and WP-B's baseline epoch guards it as it guards any save (F-1). The form clears its draft only once the new letter's id is in the garden it receives, never on the click, so a refused seal loses no words. The draft key is undated and shown only on this form, unlike W-6's goal drafts. Tabs share one drafts record, as they do today (D-34 recommends keeping it so).
- **The day.** Local midnight, the garden's calendar day. If D-24 moves the boundary, the letter follows it.

**Privacy and safety.**

- Nothing leaves the device: no new permission, request or dependency. Data processed only on the device is not "collected" ([Play Console Help](https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10787469)), so the Data safety answers (P-4) do not change.
- The words appear only in the Journal's letters. The garden card names the day it was written, never the words. `lib/summaries.ts`, `lib/gardenYear.ts`, `lib/onThisDay.ts`, `lib/journalSearch.ts`, `lib/journalExport.ts`, `lib/postcard.ts` and the calendar files never read it, and a sentinel test pins that.
- A letter let go stays in the daily copies for up to seven days and in the two pre-restore copies, as the policy already describes. Without the lock, anyone holding the unlocked device can read it, as they can the Journal; the separate "Open the letter" step keeps it off screen until asked for.
- `public/privacy.html` needs one bullet under "What the app keeps, and where". D-6 approved only the current wording, so this waits on the owner.
- Nothing about a letter is counted: no badge on the dock or More (`lib/navigation.ts`), no "unopened for N days", no reminder, no notification, nothing earned. A gentle day (`Profile.gentleDay`) holds the card. The day can always be changed, earlier or later. It is free for everyone, not behind the Garden Pass (D-79).

**How to build it.**

1. `src/types.ts`: `FutureLetter` and `Profile.futureLetters?`, with a comment on why it lives in the profile. `src/lib/letters.ts`: `changeFutureLetters(state, change)`, shaped like `changeComfortPlan` in `lib/comfortPlan.ts` (the same garden for no change, the field deleted when empty). `src/hooks/useGardenState.ts`: the action, through `update()`.
2. `src/repository/validateGarden.ts`: a lean `checkFutureLetters`, called in `checkProfile` after the comfort plan. It keeps whole any record with a string `id`, string `text` and local-date `opensOn`, so fields a later build adds survive. A full per-field check measured +264 B, 30 B over the Android limit, so trimming, lengths and unique ids live in the lazy reader.
3. `src/lib/routes.ts` and `src/App.tsx`: widen `Route.letter` to `boolean | 'later'`, and pass `onChangeFutureLetters` to `GardenView` and `JournalView`.
4. New `src/lib/futureLetters.ts` (lazy, loaded with `LetterCard` and the Journal): the limits (2,000 characters, 24 letters, five years); `lettersOf(profile)`, a defensive reader; `opensOnFor` with month-end and 29 February clamping; `returnedLetters(letters, today, gentleDay)`; and `sealLetter`, `openLetter`, `putAwayCard`, `changeDay`, `letGo` and `restore` (for Undo).
5. `src/components/garden/LetterCard.tsx` (already lazy): the returned-letter card before the weekly one, through `useLocalDate`. Styles in `letterCard.css`.
6. `src/components/journal/Letters.tsx`: lazily load two new parts. `LaterLetters.tsx` holds the waiting list, the returned and opened letters, Write back, and the support link through the comfort plan's `openSupport` path. `LetterToLater.tsx` is the form: `useDraft('future-letter', '')` with `DraftKept`, openers from new `src/data/letterOpeners.ts`, the horizons and date field, and the storage note from `lib/storage.ts`'s `storageStatus()` on the web. Styles in `journal.css`.
7. Words: a paragraph in `src/components/GuideView.tsx`'s "Letters and your year"; the `public/privacy.html` bullet once approved; `src/data/releases.ts`, a README bullet, and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`'s "Marigold's weekly letter" row.
8. Run CLAUDE.md's four checks, knip, `build:android`, and `npm run budgets` on `dist` and `--dist dist-android`.

**How it is tested.** Unit: `validateGarden.futureLetters.test.ts` keeps a well-formed list as the very same array (also through JSON), drops malformed records, carries unknown fields and repairs nothing; a fixture passes the shipped `checkProfile` untouched; `futureLetters.test.ts` clamps 31 January plus a month and 29 February plus a year, returns a letter on and after its day but not before or on a gentle day, and refuses a 25th. A sentinel run through the weekly letter, the year, On this day, search, every export format and the postcard never appears. `lockedGarden.test.ts` finds no plaintext sentinel in stored meta or drafts; `twoTabs.test.ts` sees a seal reach the other tab, and a stale seal refused with its draft kept. Component tests check the card and waiting list never contain the words. A new `e2e/letters.spec.ts` (desktop and phone) seals a letter, installs Playwright's clock at a fixed time of day past the opening date (W-15), opens it from the card, and runs axe on the form, the waiting list and an opened letter. `e2e/android.spec.ts` runs the same path and finds no read-aloud control. The Android first load stays at or under 169,245 B, or a new baseline is set on purpose under D-10.

**Risks, and what keeps them small.**

- **Claiming too much.** The benefit is small, short and partly unreplicated. No copy, Guide text or listing makes a health claim; it is offered as a keepsake.
- **A heavy letter read later.** Opening is a deliberate second step, a gentle day holds the card, the support link sits under the words, and a letter can be let go unopened.
- **A letter that feels like a task.** Nothing is counted or earned, and Later puts the card away for good.
- **Durability.** A Safari tab can be evicted and a phone can be lost. One quiet note where storage is best-effort; backups carry the letters.
- **First-load room.** About 114 to 124 B of 234 B spare. Land it after 4.0, or set a baseline under D-10; WP-F 3 would leave about 2.6 kB.
- **An older build shows nothing.** The letters are kept and come back with a newer build.
- **Profile growth.** 24 letters of 2,000 characters add at most about 48 KB to each meta write; that is why the caps exist.
- **A moved clock** can open a letter early. Harmless: any recorded `openedOn` stays opened.
- **Read-aloud creeping in later** could send the words to a network voice and break invariant 8. It stays out.

**Questions for the owner.**

- **D-79.** Build it after 4.0, free for everyone (outside the Garden Pass, D-1, and the content packs, D-46), with one new privacy-policy bullet approved as D-6 was, and about 114 to 124 B of the Android first load spent on purpose (D-10, P-18)? *Recommendation:* yes.
- **D-80.** The letter's limits and manners: a month, a season (three months), a year, or a chosen day up to five years ahead; 2,000 characters; at most 24 kept; may come back early through Change the day; its card held on a gentle day; left out of the readable Markdown and printable copies, as the comfort plan is; nothing earned. *Recommendation:* yes to each.
- **Left out unless the owner asks:** read-aloud (never in the Android app; a later PWA pilot would use only voices reporting `localService` true, and never Chrome for Android), and a calendar event for the opening day (the garden card is enough). The hour a letter opens follows D-24, which is still open.

### c.4 A garden check: kept, copied, offline, locked, and private

**In one line.** A read-only look in Settings, opened only when the gardener asks, that says in plain sentences where the garden lives and what takes its room, when it was last copied and how much has been written since, whether this version opens without a connection, whether the recovery code still opens the lock (checked without changing anything), and that every request this page made went to its own address; it stores nothing and sends nothing.

**Recommendation:** build later, after 4.0 reaches production (P-22), in two steps: first where it lives, copies and the recovery-code check; then offline and "what this page has fetched" · **Effort:** M · **First load:** 0 B on both builds, estimated, not measured. Settings is already a lazy page (`App.tsx`); the card adds about 0.2–0.4 kB gzip to its chunk, and the check itself (about 4–6 kB gzip) loads only on request · **Waits on:** D-81 and D-82. D-6's approved policy covers it as written, and D-7 changes only the address it names.

**Why it matters.** The journal's only copy is usually on one device, and the ways to lose it are silent. A best-effort origin can be evicted under storage pressure, and eviction takes all of an origin's data at once, IndexedDB and Cache Storage together ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Storage_API/Storage_quotas_and_eviction_criteria)). Safari deletes script-written storage, service-worker caches included, after seven days of Safari use with no interaction on the site ([WebKit](https://webkit.org/blog/10218/full-third-party-cookie-blocking-and-more/)). A recovery sheet can go missing, and `NO_WAY_BACK` in `lib/gardenLock.ts` already tells the gardener that nobody can open the garden once both secrets are gone. Today the answers are scattered. The protection line, usage and earlier-copies size sit at the foot of the Backup card, the photo count in another note, and the reminder appears only once a copy is 30 days old or 50 entries behind (`BACKUP_REMINDER_DAYS`, `BACKUP_REMINDER_ENTRIES` in `lib/backupReminder.ts`). The lock card has no way to confirm the sheet still works. On a Pixel 7 the Backup card starts about 9,600 px down a 12,676 px Settings page, and the lock card about 11,400 px (this review's walk), which is why this pairs with section (b)'s Settings contents (B-H-1). (b)'s B-S-3 puts a small "Keeping your garden safe" card at the top of Settings with the last copy, the earlier copies, the browser's answer and the lock, always in view. This check is the deeper look that card links to, opened only on request. The check gathers those answers in one place and adds three the app cannot give: a recovery-code check that changes nothing, so a wrong or lost sheet is found while a new code can still be made; an offline answer before a flight, which also answers W-9 for the gardener; and the page's own record of what it fetched, which shows the README's "nothing is ever requested from a third party" on screen, as WP-H 1 pins it in the test suite (F-39).

The evidence supports the need, not this design, and it is indirect. In a survey of 149 veterans with depression or anxiety, data privacy was the second most common concern about mental-health apps (59.1 %), after doubts about efficacy (71.8 %) ([Lipschitz et al., 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6367667)). In a vendor survey of 1,861 US computer owners, 54 % had lost data and 10 % backed up daily ([Backblaze, 2022](https://www.backblaze.com/blog/the-2022-backup-survey-54-report-data-loss-with-only-10-backing-up-daily/)); a vendor's numbers are weaker evidence. Two findings shape the design. A transparency dashboard left 153 people significantly less concerned, yet only 25 % would change a setting, so a self-report can reassure beyond what it proves ([Farke et al., USENIX Security 2021](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity21/presentation/farke)). Warnings shown on every visit habituate (fMRI, n = 25; field study, n = 80), so the check opens on request and never stands as a badge ([Anderson et al., JMIS 2016](https://jmis-web.org/articles/1304)). No study measures a check like this one.

**Where it works.** As of 17 November 2026. Resource Timing (Chrome 29, Firefox 35, Safari 11; [BCD](https://github.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/blob/main/api/PerformanceResourceTiming.json)) and Cache Storage (Firefox 41, Safari 11.1; [BCD](https://github.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/blob/main/api/CacheStorage.json)) are everywhere the garden runs, and reading a cache makes no request.

- **PWA on iPhone and iPad.** `persist()` and `persisted()` since iOS 15.2, `estimate()` only from iOS 17, and no `usageDetails` ([BCD](https://github.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/blob/main/api/StorageManager.json)). On 15.2 to 16.x the check measures the garden itself and gives no browser total. WebKit grants persistence by heuristics "like whether the website is opened as a Home Screen Web App", gives a Home Screen app the browser's quota, and does not evict a persistent origin ([WebKit, August 2023](https://webkit.org/blog/14403/updates-to-storage-policy/)). Home Screen apps keep their own seven-day counter ([WebKit](https://webkit.org/blog/10218/full-third-party-cookie-blocking-and-more/)). CSP reports through `ReportingObserver` arrive only from Safari 18.4 ([BCD](https://github.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/blob/main/api/ReportingObserver.json)); older versions leave that line out.
- **Android Chrome and desktop Chromium.** Everything, including the non-standard `usageDetails`, so "the app itself, kept for offline use" can be named in bytes. `persist()` is decided silently from engagement, installation or a bookmark, and notification permission, and a refusal can be asked again ([web.dev](https://web.dev/articles/persistent-storage)).
- **Desktop Firefox.** `estimate()` since 57, without `usageDetails`. `persist()` shows a permission prompt ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Storage_API/Storage_quotas_and_eviction_criteria)), so "Ask the browser again" says a question may appear. The best-effort quota is the smaller of 10 % of the disk and 10 GiB. CSP reports since Firefox 149, 24 March 2026 ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Mozilla/Firefox/Releases/149)).
- **Desktop Safari 17 and later.** As on iPhone.
- **The Android app**, a Chromium WebView at `https://localhost`. `estimate()`, `usageDetails`, Resource Timing and `ReportingObserver` all work. There is no service worker, so the offline answer is that everything is inside the app. Persistence is not a browser grant: the garden is in the app's private storage. Passkeys are never offered (`passkeysOffered()` returns false in the Android build). Play Billing runs natively and never appears in the page's record, so the check says so.
- **No platform** gives true free space (quota comes from total disk size, [MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Storage_API/Storage_quotas_and_eviction_criteria)), what extensions or the browser's own features sent, or the service worker's own fetches, which sit in the worker's timeline ([W3C Resource Timing](https://www.w3.org/TR/resource-timing/)).

**What the gardener sees.** Settings, a new card directly after "Backup and restore" (`components/backup/GardenCheck.tsx`), and one link from the "This garden stays on this device" card: "See what this page has fetched". Each part is a heading and two or three sentences, with at most one gentle action. There is no verdict, score, red or green, or "4 of 5".

1. Eyebrow "A quiet look", heading "How your garden is kept", and "See where your garden lives, when it was last copied, and whether it opens without a connection. Looking changes nothing." **Look over my garden** shows "Looking…", then moves focus to "Your garden, looked over at 9:14".
2. **Where it lives.** "This browser has agreed to keep your garden." Or: "This browser may clear your garden if it runs short of space. A backup file keeps it safe either way.", with **Ask the browser again** and the Home Screen advice Settings already gives (chosen by `lib/install.ts`), word for word. In the Android app: "Your garden lives in this app's private storage on this phone. Android does not clear it. Uninstalling the app or clearing its storage does." Then what takes the room, "about": journal, goals and garden; field book photos (12 of 50); your own images; earlier copies on this device; copies set aside, only when there are some; and the app itself. Never "plenty of room" or a percentage.
3. **Copies you keep.** "Your last copy is from 3 November. Since then you have written on 6 days." With day counts hidden or on a gentle day: "You have written since then." With none: "You have not saved a copy yet. It takes a moment, and it is the one way to bring your garden to a new device." On a desktop with a kept file, that file's own status in `AutoBackupSetting`'s words. Then: "Earlier copies on this device can put a mistake right, but they go wherever the garden goes. A backup file is the copy that survives a lost phone or a cleared browser." **Save a backup now** moves focus to the existing backup button.
4. **Without a connection.** "This version ({version}) is kept on this device, so the garden opens without a connection." Then: "Ready offline: Sunlit Meadow and Cottage Bloom, by day and by night. Woodland Brook's night painting is kept once it has been shown while you are online." On a first visit: "This visit is not kept for offline use yet. Open the garden once more while you are online, and it will be." A waiting update is named. In the Android app: "Everything the app needs is inside it, so it never needs a connection."
5. **The lock**, only when it is on. "Your garden is sealed on this device. It opens with your passcode or your recovery code", adding the passkey "for anyone who can unlock this device" where one is stored (W-12). **Check my recovery code…** is (b)'s B-S-7 check, reached from here as well as from the lock card. It opens the lock screen's 24-word field: "Type the 24 words from your recovery sheet. This only checks them: nothing is changed, and they are not kept." Then "Checking… this can take a few seconds." Right: "These words open your garden. Nothing was changed." Wrong: "These words did not open the lock. Nothing was changed. Check them against your sheet, in order. If the sheet is lost, you can make a new recovery code while the garden is open.", linked to the lock card's "Make a new recovery code…". Where a passkey is stored, **Try my passkey** opens and discards in the same way.
6. **What this page has fetched.** "Since you opened the garden at 9:14, this page has asked for 41 files, all from its own address (dionnedeh.github.io)." The paths sit behind a disclosure. Where supported: "Its own rules stopped nothing." Another host, by host only: "One request went to another address: example.com. The garden never asks for it. A browser extension can, and your browser's extension settings show which." Always: "This is the page's own record. Your browser's own features, such as an enhanced spell checker, and its extensions work outside it." The Android app adds a Play Billing sentence.
7. **Look again** and "Nothing here was saved or sent." close it.

**What it stores, and what older builds do.** Nothing. No IndexedDB key, profile field or "last checked" time: results live in component state. `DATABASE_VERSION` and `CURRENT_STATE_VERSION` both stay 8.

- **Backups (invariant 6).** The envelope and the garden inside it keep their shape, so older builds have nothing new to read or lose.
- **Reads, through paths that exist.** The in-memory garden (photos through `photosIn`), `gardenRepository.earlierCopies()`, `quarantined()` with `sizeOf`, `readBackupFile()` and `readLock()`. None opens a write transaction or moves `meta.revision`, so invariants 2, 4 and 9 are untouched. `readRaw()` is deliberately not used: it opens the database without a version, which would create an empty one if another tab had just deleted the garden.
- **The lock's sealing.** Sizes come from the open garden, and from stored records without decrypting them (`sizeOf` counts ciphertext bytes). Nothing is re-sealed. The recovery-code check never calls `writeLock` or `rekey`, so the lock record's id, stamp and wraps are the same afterwards.
- **Cross-tab.** The check is a snapshot "at 9:14" and adds no `BroadcastChannel` message. If another tab locks the garden, the lock screen replaces Settings and the check's state, typed words included, goes with it. A delete elsewhere follows the existing `garden-clearing` path; the check holds no connection of its own.
- **The backup's kind is not stored.** Naming "plain" or "protected" beside the last copy would need an optional `{ at, kind }` profile pair. Older builds keep it (`checkProfile` spreads unknown fields) but move `lastBackupAt` without it, so the pair would hide a stale kind rather than show a wrong one. The recommendation is not to add it.

**Privacy and safety.**

- **Nothing leaves the device.** Every input is local: `navigator.storage`, Cache Storage, `navigator.serviceWorker.getRegistration()` (never `update()`, which fetches `sw.js`), `performance.getEntriesByType` and a buffered `ReportingObserver`, which also sees reports made before it existed ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/ReportingObserver/ReportingObserver)). The CSP stays `connect-src 'self'`. No `report-to` or `report-uri` is ever added, because the Reporting API would then post reports to a server ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Mozilla/Firefox/Releases/149)).
- **The self-report says what it cannot see.** Resource Timing leaves out a request the CSP blocked, keeps at least 250 entries, and does not hold the worker's own fetches ([W3C](https://www.w3.org/TR/resource-timing/)). Past 250, the check vouches only for the first 250. Chrome's Enhanced spell check and Edge's Editor send typed text to their servers outside anything the page sees ([Passbolt](https://www.passbolt.com/incidents/spell-jacking-chrome-edge)), so the limits sentence always shows. "Its own address" is `location.origin`, which other pages share on GitHub Pages (F-13); D-6's passage (1) stays the honest statement until D-7.
- **The recovery-code check.** It reuses `openLock` and throws the key away. The field copies `LockScreen.tsx`'s (`autoComplete="off"`, `autoCapitalize="none"`, `autoCorrect="off"`, `spellCheck={false}`), so a spell checker cannot send the words. They are cleared on success, failure and unmount, never drafted, and never hold the auto-lock (D-5). It is offered only while the garden is unlocked, so it is no oracle for anyone without the open garden, and each try costs the full PBKDF2 derivation (D-23).
- **Tone.** The check never runs on its own and adds no badge or navigation marker. No sentence says "failed", "at risk" or "warning". "Days written since" is information, not a streak (D-39).

**How to build it.**

1. `src/lib/gardenCheck.ts` (new, lazy): `requestsThisSession(entries, origin)` (hosts only, non-http(s) ignored, a full buffer flagged), `daysWrittenSince(journal, lastBackupAt)` (distinct local days by `createdAt` across check-ins, reflections and recaps), `storageParts(...)`, and `estimateWithDetails()`, which feature-detects `usageDetails`. Nothing goes into `lib/storage.ts` or `lib/backupReminder.ts`, which ride in the first load.
2. `src/lib/offlineCheck.ts` (new). The PWA branch sits behind an inline `import.meta.env.VITE_PLATFORM !== 'android'`, so the Android bundle drops it. It finds `'workbox-precache-v2-' + scope`, the name workbox-core 7.4.1 gives the precache ([Chrome for Developers](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/workbox/modules/workbox-core)), compares its paths (ignoring `__WB_REVISION__`) with the first-load assets named in the DOM plus `index.html`, checks each owned scene's URLs from `data/backdropAssets.ts` with `caches.match`, and reports `registration.waiting`. An unknown layout says "cannot tell".
3. `src/lib/gardenLockActions.ts` (already lazy): `checkRecoveryCode(words)`, which B-S-7 adds; if this ships first, it adds it here instead, in the shape of the existing `checkPasscode`, calling `openLock(await storedLock(), { slot: 'recovery', words })` and discarding the key; and `checkPasskey()`, reusing `askPasskey` and `openLockWithPasskey`'s unwrap without touching the unlock state. Neither writes.
4. `src/components/backup/GardenCheck.tsx` and `gardenCheck.css` (new), with a link from B-S-3's `SafetyGlance.tsx` if it has shipped. It imports `gardenRepository` directly, as `EarlierCopies.tsx` does, so `App.tsx` gains no props. It reuses `storageStatus`, `requestPersistentStorage`, `formatBytes`, the reminder's month words, `homeScreenAdvice`, `isInstalledApp`, `readRecoveryCode` and `useLockState`, announces through `role="status"`, and moves focus as Settings does for `previewHeading`.
5. `src/components/SettingsView.tsx`: the card after the backup section, with `lazy(() => import('./backup/GardenCheck'))`, an `onAskStorage` that reuses `askToKeepStorage`, an `onSaveBackup` that focuses the existing button, and the privacy card's link. If WP-G 1 has split the file by then, this is one more card component.
6. Optionally, a Guide section (`#/guide/keeping` in `lib/routes.ts` and `components/GuideView.tsx`) linked from the card.
7. Docs: a row in `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`'s concern table and a line in `README.md`. `public/privacy.html` and the Data safety answers in `docs/play-store/README.md` do not change.
8. Write the tests below, then regenerate the style baselines with `UPDATE_STYLE_BASELINES=1 npx playwright test e2e/visual.spec.ts` and read the diff.
9. `npm run build && npm run budgets`, then `npm run build:android && npm run budgets -- --dist dist-android`: the first load must stay as it is, within the limit `scripts/bundle-baseline.json` sets. Then knip, CLAUDE.md's four checks, and the Android checks, since Settings has `isAndroidApp()` branches.

**How it is tested.** `lib/gardenCheck.test.ts`: hosts without paths or queries, `data:` and `blob:` ignored, a full buffer at 250, `https://localhost` as the Android origin; days counted by local day across New York's DST change; no number with counts hidden or on a gentle day; no sentence with "%", "score", "fail", "at risk", "streak", "warning" or "!". `lib/offlineCheck.test.ts` with `caches` and `serviceWorker` mocked: kept, not yet, waiting, a night painting missing, cannot tell, and `fetch` and `update` never called. `lib/gardenLockActions.test.ts`: right words resolve, wrong ones throw `WrongSecretError`, the stored lock is deep-equal before and after, and `writeLock` is never called. `GardenCheck.test.tsx`: lazy load and focus, PWA and Android words (mocked as `SettingsView.android.test.tsx` does), the field's attributes, an empty field after every outcome, and no repository write. E2e: `app.spec.ts` reads real Resource Timing ("all from its own address") with an axe sweep, and the offline relaunch test reads the offline sentence; `lock.spec.ts` checks right and wrong words, then unlocks with the same passcode and recovery code; `android.spec.ts` checks the Android words and axe.

**Risks, and what keeps them small.**

- **False reassurance.** A transparency view lowers concern whether or not that is warranted (Farke et al.), and this one cannot see extensions, the browser's features, the worker's fetches or native billing. The limits sentence always shows, and hosts are named neutrally.
- **Worry from the framing, and habituation.** On request only; no verdict, colours or badge; at most one action per part. Keep "health" out of the app and the Play listing, which `docs/play-store/README.md` files under Stress Management in the [Health apps declaration](https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/14738291).
- **Two answers that drift apart.** The Backup card's storage line, reminder and Home Screen note stay for now, and the check reuses their functions and words. Moving them into the check is a later choice, since it changes visual baselines.
- **Workbox internals.** The precache name could change with vite-plugin-pwa 2 (held in ARCHITECTURE's Toolchain table) or Workbox 8. A unit test pins it against `node_modules/workbox-core`.
- **One more typing of the 24 words.** Optional, spell check off, no draft, cleared at once. One derivation at 600,000 rounds takes a few seconds on older phones; the form says so, and D-22's check bits would catch a mistyped word first.
- **First-load room.** Android has about 245 B to spare (F-30). Editing `lib/storage.ts`, `lib/backupReminder.ts`, `App.tsx`, `hooks/*` or the `repository` chunk would spend it; step 9 measures it.
- **Test and timing cost.** New baselines, offline e2e that depends on service-worker timing, and the Android checks. Built now, it would collide with 4.0's screenshots (P-5).

**Questions for the owner.**

- **D-81.** Build "How your garden is kept" as its own Settings card after Backup and restore, after 4.0 reaches production (P-22), in two steps? Hide "days written since" when day counts are hidden or on a gentle day? Link B-S-3's glance card to it? *Recommendation:* yes to all three. The recovery-code check is B-S-7's, so D-68 already covers its reminder. D-35 recommends leaving two counts on a gentle day because neither is the day's Sunlight; this one reads closer to a streak, which D-39 recommends keeping retired. Never call it a "health check" in the app or the listing (D-3's audience).
- **D-82.** Include "What this page has fetched", a self-report with its limits always shown, where "its own address" is the shared `github.io` origin until D-7, and the Android app adds a Play Billing sentence in the words D-6 approved? *Recommendation:* yes, in the second step and worded as here. If its limits ever have to be softened to fit, leave the part out.

### c.5 Open the locked garden with a fingerprint or face, in the Android app

**In one line.** An optional fourth way in to the garden lock, in the Android app only: a 32-byte secret sealed by an Android Keystore key that only a Class 3 fingerprint or face can release, through `BiometricPrompt` with a `CryptoObject`, so the passcode is typed less often but is no less needed.

**Recommendation:** build later, as an early 4.x release once 4.0 is live on Google Play (P-22), not in 4.0 · **Effort:** L · **First load:** 0 B on both builds, estimated: `LockGate.tsx` already loads `LockScreen` and `LockWatch` lazily, `LockSettings` sits inside the lazy Settings page, and the new `lib/deviceUnlock.ts` is imported only from the lock's components, behind an inline `VITE_PLATFORM` check. The lazy lock chunk grows by an estimated 0.3 to 0.5 kB gzip in both builds and the Android-only lock chunks by about 1 to 2 kB more, not measured · **Waits on:** D-6 (new privacy and SECURITY.md wording, approved afresh), P-4 (declarations checked against the new permission), D-83 to D-85

**Why it matters.** With the lock on in the Android app, the gardener types the passcode every time the garden opens, after every auto-lock too, because passkeys are switched off there (`passkeysOffered()` in `lib/passkey.ts` returns false in the Android build). That friction pushes people towards a short passcode, a long auto-lock or no lock at all. The evidence is about locks in general, not this app, and some of it is old. In a field study of 52 people, unlocking took 2.9 % of phone time, and PIN or pattern users judged the lock unnecessary in 24.1 % of situations ([Harbach et al., SOUPS 2014](https://www.usenix.org/conference/soups2014/proceedings/presentation/harbach)). In a 10-person lab study and a 198-person survey, fingerprint users rated fingerprint unlock both more convenient and more secure than a PIN ([Bhagavatula et al., USEC 2015](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss2015/ndss-2015-usec-programme/biometric-authentication-iphone-and-android-usability-perceptions-and-influences-adoption/)). In mHealth, a lack of security features was a barrier, and "strong but easy-to-use security features" might encourage use ([Zhou et al., JMIR mHealth 2019](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2019/4/e11223)). Typing passwords on a phone is slower and more error-prone, and phone passwords were weaker ([Melicher et al., CHI 2016](https://dblp1.uni-trier.de/rec/conf/chi/MelicherKSKSUBC16.html)), so a passcode typed rarely can afford to be longer. No study shows that biometric unlock itself improves wellbeing. The direct gain is privacy with less friction, level with what the PWA's passkeys already give.

**Where it works.**

- **The Android app** (Capacitor 8.5.3, minSdk 24, targetSdk 36). Offered on Android 11 (API 30) and later, 88.2 % of active devices on 1 October 2026 ([Google's data, via Composables](https://composables.com/android-distribution-chart)), and only with a Class 3 biometric enrolled: Class 2 "can integrate with BiometricPrompt, but cannot integrate with Keystore" ([AOSP](https://source.android.com/docs/security/features/biometric/measure)). That is most fingerprint sensors. Face counts only where the phone rates it Class 3: Pixel 8 and later, but not Pixel 7, by press coverage of Google's announcement ([9to5Google, October 2023](https://9to5google.com/2023/10/04/pixel-8-face-unlock/)). The platform `BiometricPrompt` needs no new library. `androidx.biometric`'s only stable release is still 1.1.0 (January 2021; 1.4.0-beta01 came out on 4 November 2026), and some Android 9 and 10 phones are known to present a weak biometric for crypto ([release notes](https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/biometric)). StrongBox holds the key where the phone has one, the TEE otherwise, and a software-only key is refused ([Android Keystore](https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/keystore)). On Android 16 a person can turn biometrics off for apps ([`BiometricPrompt`](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/hardware/biometrics/BiometricPrompt)), and the option then does not show.
- **The PWA on iPhone, Android Chrome and desktop browsers.** Unchanged. They keep `lib/passkey.ts`'s PRF passkeys where the browser offers PRF, and the bundler drops every Android branch.
- **The `android-webview` e2e project.** Capacitor's web fallback has no `GardenUnlock`, so the option never shows and today's lock flow runs as it is.
- **Considered and rejected:** WebView passkeys through `androidx.webkit` 1.12+. They need Digital Asset Links with "a website that your app owns" ([Android Developers](https://developer.android.com/identity/sign-in/credential-manager-webview)), but the app is served from `https://localhost` (`capacitor.config.ts`) and the PWA's own domain waits on D-7. They would also bring Google's `androidx.credentials` and Play services code into the app, and a synced passkey leaves the device.

**What the gardener sees.** Settings → Privacy → The garden lock (`components/lock/LockSettings.tsx`) and the lock screen (`components/lock/LockScreen.tsx`), in the Android build only, and only once the phone reports a Class 3 biometric.

1. Beside "Change my passcode…" sits **Open with fingerprint or face…**. Its dialog says: "Your phone can open the garden with a fingerprint or face it already knows, as well as with your passcode. Anyone whose fingerprint or face this phone accepts could open it too, so turn this on only if that is just you."
2. The passcode is typed and proved first, as for a passkey. Android's own prompt then reads "Open your garden", with **Use my passcode**. Afterwards: "Your fingerprint or face can now open the garden. Your passcode and recovery code still work too, so keep your recovery sheet safe." If the passcode is short, one more line: "Now that you will type it less often, a longer passphrase, a few words only you would put together, is stronger."
3. On the lock screen, under "Open my garden", a secondary button: **Open with fingerprint or face**. Focus stays in the passcode field and nothing opens unasked (D-84). While it works: "Opening your garden…". Cancelling says nothing.
4. When it cannot help, one calm line and never an attempt count. Lockout (5 failed tries, then 30 s, per `BiometricPrompt`): "Your phone has paused fingerprint and face checks for a little while. Your passcode opens the garden." A newly enrolled fingerprint: "This phone's fingerprints or face have changed, so they no longer open the garden. Your passcode does, and you can set this up again in Settings." A new phone: "This phone hasn't been set up to open the garden with a fingerprint or face. Your passcode opens it."
5. Now and then (D-84; seven days suggested, as Apple asks for the passcode after 156 hours without it, [Apple Platform Security](https://support.apple.com/guide/security/sec9479035f1/web)): "It's been a while, so your garden asks for your passcode today. That keeps it fresh in your mind."
6. **Stop opening with fingerprint or face** deletes the phone's key at once: "Done. Your fingerprint or face no longer opens the garden." The small print and the Guide's lock paragraph (`GuideView.tsx:342`) name the fingerprint only while it is on.

**What it stores, and what older builds do.**

- **Shape.** `LockRecord` in `lib/gardenLock.ts` gains one optional field, `device?: DeviceWrap`: the usual slot (public half, sealed private half, sealed data key) plus `alias` (the Keystore alias), `keyIv` (12 B), `sealedSecret` (48 B: the secret and its tag) and `lockId` (the record id its data key was sealed for). `passkey?` is optional inside `version: 1` in the same way, so the version stays 1. No new store: `DATABASE_VERSION` and `CURRENT_STATE_VERSION` stay 8, and `validateGarden` is untouched, since the lock record is not part of the garden. Nothing in it is secret: the wrapped secret is useless without the phone's hardware and the person.
- **Re-keys.** Sealing to a slot's public half needs no secret, so `withNewDataKey` seals each new data key to the device slot without a prompt. A new passcode or recovery code, or a removed passkey, keeps the fingerprint working.
- **Backups (invariant 6).** Unchanged. Plain and protected backups and move files never carry the lock. Safe mode's "Download what's stored" holds the raw record, device fields included, but `readLockedCopy` (`lib/lockedCopy.ts`) rebuilds a record from the passcode and recovery slots alone, so every copy opens as before and the device fields are inert off the phone.
- **Older builds (3.4 to 4.0)** ignore `device`. A re-key there spreads the whole record (`{ ...base, … }` in `withNewDataKey`), so the slot it carries still holds the old data key. A newer build sees `lockId` ≠ `id` and does not offer it, and AES-GCM's label check would fail closed if asked. Settings offers to set it up again. Turning the lock off and on in an old build drops the field, and the orphaned alias is pruned at the next setup. The slot is only ever an extra way in, so nothing a gardener wrote can be lost.
- **The lock's sealing.** No change to `repository/sealedStores.ts` or `repository/gardenSwitch.ts`. Adding is a `writeLock` with a new stamp, refused if the record changed since it was read, as `addPasskey` is. Removing deletes the Keystore entry first, which alone kills the slot, then writes the record without it (a full re-key is D-85).
- **Cross-tab and transfer.** Every change calls `tellOtherTabs({ type: 'garden-lock' })`, on which `LockScreen` already re-reads. A phone-to-phone transfer (`data_extraction_rules.xml`) brings the record but not the non-exportable key, so the new phone shows the note in step 4. "Delete all local data" and "Delete this garden…" also forget the alias, best effort.

**Privacy and safety.**

- **Nothing new leaves the device.** `BiometricPrompt` is system UI, and Keystore key material "never enters the application process" ([Android Keystore](https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/keystore)). The app receives a released cipher, never a fingerprint or face, and uses no network or Play services code. Data processed only on the device is not "collected" ([Play Console Help](https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10787469)), so the Data safety answers do not change. The manifest gains `USE_BIOMETRIC`, a normal install-time permission with no prompt ([Manifest.permission](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/Manifest.permission#USE_BIOMETRIC), checked 17 November 2026). The research read only part of Play's [sensitive-permissions policy](https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/16558241) and found no biometric entry there; P-4 checks it again.
- **Crypto-bound, not event-bound.** The secret exists outside the hardware only after the Keystore releases a per-use AES-256-GCM key: timeout 0, `BIOMETRIC_STRONG` only, invalidated by a new enrolment ([`KeyGenParameterSpec.Builder`](https://developer.android.com/reference/android/security/keystore/KeyGenParameterSpec.Builder), [biometric guide](https://developer.android.com/identity/sign-in/biometric-auth)). A patched success callback, the bypass [OWASP MASTG](https://mas.owasp.org/MASTG/demos/android/MASVS-AUTH/MASTG-DEMO-0091/MASTG-DEMO-0091/) describes, opens nothing.
- **People, not cryptography, are the real risk.** Anyone whose fingerprint or face the phone accepts can open the garden. `LockSettings`' Android small print promises that "on a lost or shared phone your journal can't be read without your passcode or recovery code", and in consultations with 31 survivors of intimate-partner abuse, eight had passwords known or guessable by the abuser ([Freed et al., CSCW 2019](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10192524)). So it is off by default, the phone's own PIN or pattern (`DEVICE_CREDENTIAL`) never opens it, and a fingerprint added after setup does not work.
- **It does not harden a weak passcode.** Each slot opens the garden on its own, so the lock is as strong as its weakest slot, and the passcode slot is exactly as guessable as before. One RTX 4090 tries about 14,800 passcodes a second at 600,000 rounds ([hashcat benchmark](https://gist.github.com/Chick3nman/32e662a5bb63bc4f51b847bb422222fd), scaled): every 6-digit passcode in about 68 s, every 6-lowercase-letter one in about 6 hours (F-14 gives a faster card's figure). The device slot itself cannot be guessed offline (256 bits under a key that never leaves the hardware). The lever for a weak passcode stays F-14's WP-C 1, and a long passphrase is easier to live with once it is typed rarely. A Keystore "pepper" on the passcode slot was assessed and should not be built: apps get no hardware throttling, and safe mode's copy and a phone-to-phone transfer would then open only with the recovery code. D-23 is untouched.
- **Two small limits.** The secret crosses the bridge as a base64 string that JavaScript cannot wipe; it is decoded at once and the bytes are zeroed after HKDF. Any script in the WebView could call the plugin, which the CSP's `'self'` and the bundled-only content contain.

**How to build it.**

1. Record the owner's answers (D-83 to D-85) in `docs/plans/future-update.md`, and schedule the work after P-22.
2. `android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml`: add `USE_BIOMETRIC` with a comment in the file's style. New, pure and JVM-testable `android/app/src/main/java/io/github/dionnedeh/butterflygarden/unlock/UnlockRules.java`: the alias format, the 12-byte IV and 48-byte checks, the AAD bytes, and one mapping from prompt and Keystore errors to reasons (dismissed, lockout, invalidated, missing, unavailable, not-enabled-for-apps).
3. New `unlock/GardenUnlockPlugin.java` (`@CapacitorPlugin(name = "GardenUnlock")`): `status`, `enrol`, `open` and `forget`. StrongBox first, the TEE on `StrongBoxUnavailableException`, and a key below `TRUSTED_ENVIRONMENT` refused. The prompt allows `BIOMETRIC_STRONG` only. Prompts run on the UI thread and Keystore work off it, and the plugin wakes nothing until called, as `GardenBillingPlugin` does. Register it in `MainActivity.java` beside the other two.
4. New `src/lib/deviceUnlock.ts`, with an inline `VITE_PLATFORM === 'android'` check in every export: status, enrol, ask and forget over `inAppPlugin('GardenUnlock')` from `lib/native.ts`. Rejections and Capacitor's `UNIMPLEMENTED` map to `{ ok: false, reason }`.
5. `src/lib/gardenLock.ts`: `DeviceWrap`, `LockRecord.device?`, a `'device'` slot, `withNewDataKey` sealing to it and stamping `lockId`, and `deviceWrappingKey` (HKDF-SHA-256, info `${LOCK_FORMAT}:device`, like `passkeyWrappingKey`), `withDeviceKey`, `openLockWithDevice`, `withoutDeviceKey`, `isCurrentDeviceSlot`.
6. `src/lib/gardenLockActions.ts`: `addDeviceUnlock` (passcode proved first), `unlockWithDevice`, `removeDeviceUnlock` (key forgotten first) and `deviceSlotOf` (prunes a stale slot), each calling `tellOtherTabs()`.
7. `LockScreen.tsx`, `LockSettings.tsx`, and `LockChange.tsx` with a `'device'` kind whose `AddDeviceUnlock`, modelled on `AddPasskey`, carries `data-auto-lock-waits` while the prompt is up, so `LockWatch` does not start the clock if a maker's prompt stops the activity.
8. Words and docs: `GuideView.tsx`, `data/releases.ts`, SECURITY.md (the ways in, three native plugins in scope), `public/privacy.html` (replacing "(not in the Android app)" and the no-way-back passage, only after approval, with W-12's `NO_WAY_BACK` settled in the same change), ARCHITECTURE, `docs/play-store/README.md` and `docs/android-play-plan.md`.
9. CLAUDE.md's four checks, `npm run budgets` on `dist` and `dist-android`, `npm run knip`, then the full Android checks.

**How it is tested.** Unit: a device slot opens the same data key as the passcode, and a wrong secret throws `WrongSecretError`; every re-key carries the slot without the device secret; a slot carried by an older build (`lockId` ≠ `id`) is not offered and fails closed; safe mode's copy of such a lock opens with the passcode and with the recovery code; the passcode is proved before enrolment, and a stale stamp is refused; each native reason maps to its result. A build assertion finds no `GardenUnlock` in the web build. Component tests mock `lib/deviceUnlock`, as `lib/passkey` is mocked today: the button shows only for a current slot, a cancel shows nothing, focus stays put, and `LockWatch` holds while the prompt is up. `e2e/android.spec.ts` checks the option is absent without the plugin, with axe. `UnlockRulesTest` runs on the JVM, like `PassEntitlementTest`. No container or CI can drive a real biometric, so acceptance is a device matrix recorded as P-14 asks for passkeys: a Pixel 8 or later, a Samsung with fingerprint (whose face is not Class 3), and an Android 11 phone. Each runs setup, unlock, cancel, lockout, a new fingerprint, removing the screen lock, a transfer, TalkBack, and an auto-lock during setup.

**Risks, and what keeps them small.**

- **A false sense of security.** A gardener may think the fingerprint protects a weak passcode. The words never say so, and WP-C 1's advice sits beside it.
- **Shared phones and coercion.** Off by default, biometric only, invalidated by a new enrolment, and the setup words say who else could open it.
- **A forgotten passcode.** Someone who always uses a fingerprint can forget it, and `NO_WAY_BACK` then rests on the recovery sheet. The occasional passcode ask (D-84) keeps it in mind.
- **Makers differ.** Prompts, StrongBox quirks, and keys "irreversibly invalidated" when the screen lock is removed. The passcode always remains, and every failure falls back to it.
- **A wider surface.** A third native plugin and a new permission. SECURITY.md and the P-4 declarations are updated in the same change.
- **Policy timing.** The privacy policy was approved as written on 3 November 2026 (D-6). New wording folded into 4.0 would put the first Play release at risk, which is why this waits.

**Questions for the owner.**

- **D-83.** Build it as an early 4.x release once 4.0 is live (P-22): off by default, biometric only (`BIOMETRIC_STRONG`, never the phone's PIN, pattern or password), Android 11 and later through the platform API, with new `privacy.html` and SECURITY.md wording approved afresh beyond D-6? *Recommendation:* yes to each. Android 9 and 10 through `androidx.biometric` would reach 97.0 % of devices, not 88.2 %, but with a 2021 library and known vendor bugs.
- **D-84.** Ask for the passcode after seven days without it? That keeps one date in the lock record's readable part, which SECURITY.md's list would then name. And should the prompt open by itself when the app comes back, or only on a tap? *Recommendation:* yes to the seven days; only on a tap.
- **D-85.** Turning it off: delete the phone's key and drop the slot, or also re-seal the garden under a new data key, as removing a passkey does? And confirm there is no Keystore "pepper" on the passcode slot? *Recommendation:* either is safe. Deleting the key is enough on its own, since it can never be used again; a re-seal buys one rule for every way in. The research does not lean. No pepper, on either answer.

### c.6 Share a photo to the field book, and a field book that travels

**In one line.** Send a photo from Photos, the gallery or the camera to The Butterfly Garden and land in "Log a sighting" with it already prepared, location stripped as today; and save the field book as a CSV whose first eight columns follow iNaturalist's import, which the gardener uploads themselves, so the app itself sends nothing anywhere.

**Recommendation:** build later, after 4.0 is submitted, in three steps: the Android share in 4.1, the CSV with (b)'s B-J-3 once D-25 is answered, and the PWA share target last · **Effort:** L · **First load:** 0 B on the Android build (`dist-android`), estimated, not measured: everything that receives the photo is lazy, and the native side moves the page to the existing `#/journal` route itself, so neither `lib/routes.ts` nor `App.tsx` changes. About 100 to 150 B gzip on the web build, estimated, inside its roughly 800 B of room. The CSV adds 0 B to both · **Waits on:** D-25 (the CSV), D-6 (one added privacy-policy sentence), D-86, D-87

**Why it matters.** Today a gardener who photographs a butterfly must open the garden, go to the Journal, open the field notes, open the field book, press "Log a sighting", then "Add a photo" and find the picture again. Sharing from the gallery or the camera's own Share button skips most of that at the moment of noticing, which is when the field book matters. The evidence for noticing nature is reasonable. In an RCT of 582 adults, an app prompting people to note good things about urban nature gave sustained wellbeing gains at one month against a built-space control; its participants typed what they noticed rather than photographing it ([McEwan et al. 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6765898)). In a one-week randomised experiment with 500 people, noticing and citizen-science recording both raised nature connectedness and happiness; recording gave more sense of making a difference but less calm and joy ([Pocock et al. 2023](https://nora.nerc.ac.uk/id/eprint/535393/)). Friction is a real limit: in the McEwan group's 30-day feasibility study, 885 people downloaded the app, 50 gave data at the end, and participants made a mean of 6 observations each ([McEwan et al. 2021](https://eprints.whiterose.ac.uk/170970/)). No study measures whether a share-sheet entry makes people log more; that link is an inference. An 8-month RCT of biodiversity citizen science (n = 70) raised nature relatedness and self-efficacy, with no measurable change on other wellbeing or anxiety scales ([Eichholtzer et al. 2024](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:37876142%20AND%20SRC:MED&format=json&resultType=core)).

The CSV's scientific value is low by design, and the words must say so. The garden never reads location and strips it from photos, so every exported sighting reaches iNaturalist with no coordinates and no photo, and iNaturalist keeps it as a casual, non-verifiable record until the gardener adds both there ([iNaturalist Help](https://help.inaturalist.org/support/solutions/articles/151000169936)). UK schemes such as iRecord need a grid reference and a recorder ([iRecord](https://irecord.org.uk/node/9709)), and most of the field guide's species are North American. The CSV is a life list the gardener owns and can carry, not a contribution to science by itself.

**Where it works.** As of 17 November 2026, from [MDN browser-compat-data 8.1.9](https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/@mdn/browser-compat-data/data.json) (12 November 2026, `manifests.webapp.share_target`, still marked experimental). A web app is offered in the share sheet only once it is installed, and its service worker, not the page, receives the shared file ([Chrome for Developers](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/capabilities/web-apis/web-share-target)).

- **The Android app** (Capacitor 8.5.3, minSdk 24, targetSdk 36). An `ACTION_SEND` intent filter for `image/jpeg`, `image/png` and `image/webp` works on every supported version ([Android Developers](https://developer.android.com/training/sharing/receive)) and needs no media permission, because the sender grants a read on one content URI ([Play Console Help](https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/14115180)). `MainActivity` is already exported and `singleTask`, and Capacitor's `BridgeActivity` passes a share that arrives while the app runs to each plugin's `handleOnNewIntent` (checked in the installed 8.5.3 source; [Capacitor](https://github.com/ionic-team/capacitor/blob/main/android/capacitor/src/main/java/com/getcapacitor/Plugin.java)). Android WebView has no web share target, so the app needs the native path.
- **Android Chrome and Samsung Internet (the PWA).** Chrome 76+ and Samsung Internet 12+, installed only. An existing install gets the share target only after Chrome re-reads the manifest (at most once a day) and re-mints the WebAPK, which waits until the garden's windows are closed and the phone is charging on Wi-Fi ([web.dev](https://web.dev/articles/manifest-updates)). Firefox for Android parses the member and does nothing with it.
- **Desktop browsers.** Installed Chrome and Edge 89+. Windows lists the PWA in its own share sheet ([Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/windows/apps/develop/windows-integration/integrate-sharesheet-pwa)), and Chromium shipped it on ChromeOS; macOS is not documented and is treated as absent. Firefox has none.
- **The PWA on iPhone and iPad, and Safari on the Mac.** Nothing. WebKit's bug has been open and unassigned since 13 February 2019, last modified 23 May 2026 ([WebKit 194593](https://bugs.webkit.org/show_bug.cgi?id=194593)), and WebKit's recorded position since March 2023 is neutral, with concerns about spoofed names and icons ([WebKit standards positions #11](https://github.com/WebKit/standards-positions/issues/11)). "Add a photo" in the form, which already offers Photo Library and Take Photo, stays the way in.
- **The CSV** works wherever the Journal's readable copies already work: a download in browsers, and Android's save screen through `GardenFilesPlugin.saveDocument` in the app. iNaturalist's import page sits behind a bot challenge and could not be read. Its columns come from users in May 2025: eight read by position (taxon name, date observed, description, place name, latitude, longitude, tags, geoprivacy), only the taxon required, header names ignored and extra columns silently ignored ([iNaturalist Forum](https://forum.inaturalist.org/t/csv-import-observations-page/65768)). That is unverified at source, so D-87 asks for a trial upload.

**What the gardener sees.** It lives in the Journal's existing field book (`components/sightings/`) and its "Take your words with you" card (`components/JournalExport.tsx`).

1. In Photos, the gallery or the camera, the gardener taps Share and chooses The Butterfly Garden.
2. The garden comes forward, at its lock screen first if the lock is on. The Journal opens with the field notes open and "Log a sighting" showing. "Preparing your photo…" is announced, then the preview appears with the line the form already has: "A smaller copy stays on this device and in your backups. Nothing about where it was taken is kept."
3. One new line sits under the heading: "Your photo is here. What did you see?" The gardener picks the butterfly or plant, keeps today or changes the day, writes a few words if they like, and presses Save sighting. The field book says "Saved to your field book." and focus moves to the new entry, as today.
4. Cancel or Back puts the form away, and the field book says "The photo was not kept." Nothing is stored.
5. A photo that cannot be used gets words the form already says: "Choose a JPEG, PNG or WebP image from your device.", "That image is larger than 10 MB. Try a smaller copy.", the full-field-book line at 50 photos (the form still opens, so the sighting can be saved without it), or the "not kept" note where storage refuses Blobs (D-9). With no garden planted yet, the welcome says "Plant your garden first, then share the photo again."
6. In "Take your words with you", once the field book holds a sighting, a fourth button, **Field book as CSV**, beside an unticked "Include my notes" with the hint "Notes you include become public if you upload this file to a nature site." It saves `butterfly-garden-field-book-YYYY-MM-DD.csv` and says "Your download has started.", or "Saved." or "Nothing was saved." through Android's save screen, as the other copies do.
7. "Share with a nature project, if you like" (`NatureProjects.tsx`) gets one honest line: "You can save your field book as a CSV under Take your words with you and upload it to iNaturalist yourself. Your sightings arrive there without a place or a photo, so iNaturalist keeps them as casual records until you add those there."

**What it stores, and what older builds do.** No stored data changes. A sighting made from a shared photo has exactly today's `Sighting` shape: a Blob re-encoded as WebP or JPEG, at most 400 KB and 1,024 px, checked by `isSightingPhoto`. `AppState`, the state version and `DATABASE_VERSION` 8 are unchanged, with no migration and no change to `validateGarden`.

- **Invariant 6.** The backup file and its envelope are unchanged; the photo travels as base64 through `lib/sightingPhotos.ts`, as now. The CSV is a read-only copy built from the garden in memory. Even the "Include my notes" choice is not kept.
- **Older builds.** A sighting from a shared photo cannot be told from one added through the file input, so 3.3 and later read and write it unchanged, and nothing can be dropped silently.
- **The lock's sealing.** The shared bytes are written nowhere before Save. The save goes through `useGardenState`'s `changeSightings`, then `usePersistence`, then `gardenRepository`, which seals each part while the lock is on. While locked, the lock screen shows first and the photo waits in memory. The open form holds the auto-lock, within D-5's limit, and is named in `data-lock-loses` as today. A CSV can be made only while the garden is open, because the Journal is behind `LockGate`.
- **Cross-tab.** A share opens or navigates one window. The save takes the existing revision check, a conflicting tab gets `SIGHTING_NOT_SAVED` ("The garden changed in another tab, so this sighting was not saved.") through `FieldBookActions.photoSaved`, and other tabs receive the sighting through the existing sync.
- **The worker and the manifest** are versioned outside the garden, and under `registerType: 'prompt'` a re-minted WebAPK can carry a new manifest while an old worker is still in charge. So the POST handler ships one release before `share_target` appears in `src/pwaManifest.ts`; otherwise the share would reach GitHub Pages, which cannot take a POST.

**Privacy and safety.** Nothing new leaves the device through the app. The share flows inward, and the CSV leaves only when the gardener saves it and uploads it themselves.

- **Location in camera files.** Raw photos carry GPS EXIF. The shared bytes are held in memory only (in the worker until the page asks, then the page, or a field in the native plugin), never in Cache Storage, IndexedDB, the app's cache directory or its FileProvider path. They pass through the existing `prepareSightingPhoto`, which redraws only the pixels at 1,024 px or less and keeps nothing of the original. Under the lock, WP-C 6's removal of `photoCheck`'s unsealed trial write (F-20) should land first.
- **Confused-deputy URIs on Android** ([Android Developers](https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/risks/content-resolver)). Accept only `content://`. Refuse `file://` and any authority belonging to this app, including its own FileProvider of camera captures. Require a read grant (`checkUriPermission`), read through a stream capped at 10 MB, accept only the three MIME types, and check the image header again in `lib/imageImport.ts`.
- **Text from other apps.** A share's title, text and URL are ignored, so nothing from another app is typed into the gardener's notes.
- **The CSV.** Notes written in a private journal become public if uploaded, so they stay out unless ticked. Geoprivacy is prefilled "obscured", so a place the gardener adds later on iNaturalist is blurred by default. Tags stay blank, so no public record names a self-care app. No ids, `createdAt` or photo bytes go in. Every field passes the existing `csvField` guard against formulas and for RFC 4180 quoting ([OWASP](https://owasp.org/www-community/attacks/CSV_Injection)), with WP-C 6's full-width folding (F-20).
- **Play.** No new permission. Data safety does not change, since data processed only on the device need not be declared ([Play Console Help](https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10787469)), but the merged manifest is read again (P-4). The approved privacy policy needs one sentence, under D-6's process: "If you share a photo to the garden from another app, it is treated exactly like one you add in the field book: redrawn on your device, with nothing about where it was taken kept, and nothing is sent anywhere."
- **Tone.** Never imply that a sighting "counts for science". No counts, streaks or "upload more". The garden never reads location, even "to make records research grade". As today, and as D-21 recommends, it never opens the camera itself and asks for no camera permission.

**How to build it.**

1. **The CSV**, once D-25 is answered. It is (b)'s B-J-3 "Field book as CSV", built once: B-J-3's `sightingsCsv()` in `src/lib/journalExport.ts` takes these columns and the notes choice, `sightingsCsv(state, { notes })`, reusing `csvField`, the BOM and CRLF. Header: iNaturalist's eight in order (Taxon name, Date observed, Description, Place name, Latitude, Longitude, Tags, Geoprivacy), then Common name, What it was, Photo kept in the garden. Rows come newest first (`newestFirst`, `lib/sightings.ts`). The taxon is `data/content.ts`'s `scientificName`, with a trailing " spp." cut to the genus. Place, coordinates and tags are blank, geoprivacy is "obscured", and a subject this build does not know is skipped. If D-67 gives sightings their own "Where, in your own words" field, it fills Place name only when "Include my notes" is ticked, since it is the gardener's own words about a place.
2. `src/components/JournalExport.tsx`: a `fieldBook` format ("Field book as CSV", `text/csv`) offered when there are sightings, sightings counted in `isEmpty`, and the checkbox with its hint. `src/components/sightings/NatureProjects.tsx`: the one honest line. On Android this goes through `saveDocument`, so it needs WP-E 1's truncating save (F-9) before a second CSV is saved over the first.
3. **Android, in 4.1.** An `<intent-filter>` on `.MainActivity` in `android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml`: action `SEND`, category `DEFAULT`, the three image types, never `image/*` or `*/*`.
4. New `android/.../SharedPhoto.java`: pure rules with a JVM test, kept apart as `DailyEvent` is: content URIs only, not this app's own authority, a read grant, the three types, and a stream capped at 10 MB (`CUSTOM_BACKDROP_LIMITS.maxSourceBytes`).
5. `GardenFilesPlugin.java`: in `load()` (cold start) and `handleOnNewIntent()` (warm), read an `ACTION_SEND` once into a private in-memory `pendingShared`, then replace the activity's intent so a recreate cannot replay it. Move the page to `#/journal` with `bridge.eval` (after `onPageLoaded` on a cold start), call `notifyListeners('photoShared', {}, true)`, and add `takeSharedPhoto()`, which answers `{ base64, mimeType }` once, then `{}`.
6. New `src/lib/sharedPhotoNative.ts`, imported only by lazy chunks and deliberately not added to `src/lib/native.ts`, which is in Android's first load. `JournalView.tsx` takes a pending photo on mount and on `photoShared`, opens the field notes and hands it to `FieldBook.tsx` (`sharedPhoto?: File`). `SightingForm.tsx` gets `initialFile?: File`, which runs the existing `choosePhoto` on mount. WP-A 7 (F-11) lands first, because a form that opens straight into "Preparing your photo…" is exactly where the invisible-dialog defect bites.
7. **The PWA, release N.** `src/sw/shareTarget.ts`, a pure POST-only handler with its own `tsconfig.sw.json`: a POST to `share-photo` gets a 303 to `?shared-photo`; the first allowed file of 10 MB or less is held in memory, keyed by `resultingClientId`, given only to that client when it says it is ready, and dropped after 60 s. Workbox routes answer only GET by default, so the generated worker never answers this POST ([Workbox](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/workbox/modules/workbox-routing)).
8. `vite.config.ts`: a small plugin emits the handler as `share-target-<buildId>.js`, added through `generateSW`'s `importScripts` ([Workbox](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/workbox/modules/workbox-build)) and kept out of the precache, with every other option unchanged. An `injectManifest` migration would rebuild the precache, the navigation fallback and its denylist, the runtime caches and `SKIP_WAITING` by hand ([vite-plugin-pwa](https://vite-pwa-org.netlify.app/guide/inject-manifest.html)) while vite-plugin-pwa is held (W-3). New `src/lib/sharedPhotoWeb.ts` takes the file and replaces the address with `#/journal`; `src/main.tsx` imports it behind an inline `VITE_PLATFORM` check, before `LockGate`, so a locked garden still catches the photo.
9. **Release N+1.** `share_target` in `src/pwaManifest.ts`: POST, `multipart/form-data`, one `files` param named `photo` that accepts the three types and four extensions, and no title, text or url ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Progressive_web_apps/Manifest/Reference/share_target)).
10. Docs: the README, ARCHITECTURE's field-book and installed-PWA rows, `public/privacy.html` (D-6), `docs/play-store/README.md` (P-4) and a line in the device checklists (P-20). Then CLAUDE.md's four checks, `npm run budgets` for both builds, and the Android checks.
